Women’s tenure security is disproportionately eroded because (a) property titles are overwhelmingly held in male names — Census data indicates only a small fraction of urban residential titles list a woman as sole or joint owner; (b) compensation calculations rely on market value of the original plot, ignoring the loss of home‑based livelihoods such as tailoring, food processing or childcare that women operate from the dwelling; and (c) the reconstituted plot is often allocated to the “head of household” as defined by revenue records, which defaults to the eldest male.

Judicial directions have emphasised equitable redistribution and joint titling where original titles were joint, but implementation remains uneven. Studies of TPS in major cities find that a low share of reconstituted plots are issued in joint names, and women’s access to credit against new titles remains constrained by banking practices requiring male co‑owner signatures. The National Urban Livelihoods Mission (NULM) 2013 guidelines recognise home‑based work as a livelihood category, yet convergence with TPS is absent in most city development plans. A “livelihood impact assessment” (LIA) mandated under the 2015 amendment to the Gujarat Act has seen limited compliance, with few submissions and none disaggregating impacts by gender.

Policy levers that could improve outcomes include: (1) mandatory joint titling at the point of reconstitution, enforced through conditional grants from the 15th Finance Commission’s urban transformation window; (2) earmarking a share of the betterment levy for a women’s livelihood restoration fund managed by the city‑level Self‑Help Group federation; and (3) integrating gender‑disaggregated indicators — such as “percentage of women plot‑holders with independent credit access” — into the Urban Climate Resilience Index (UCRI) so that performance‑linked fiscal transfers reward compliance.

Globalisation and the Restructuring of Public Sector Employment: Gendered Outcomes

The liberalisation wave that began in 1991 triggered a systematic restructuring of public sector undertakings (PSUs) and government departments through disinvestment, voluntary retirement schemes (VRS) and contractualisation. Official data indicates that the regular workforce in central PSUs declined sharply between 1991 and 2020, while contract and casual workers increased substantially. Women, who constituted a notable share of the regular PSU workforce in 1991 (largely in clerical, nursing and teaching cadres), experienced a sharper decline in regular employment share, while their share in contract work rose. The Sixth Pay Commission (2006) recommended “equal pay for work of equal value,” but the recommendation was not extended to contract workers, leaving a significant gender pay gap for comparable tasks in the same establishment (Labour Bureau estimates).

The Supreme Court ruling in Secretary, Ministry of Defence v. Babita Puniya (2020) granted permanent commission to women officers in the Indian Army, establishing a precedent for non‑discriminatory service conditions. However, the judgment did not address the broader public sector where the “contractualisation” route bypasses the constitutional guarantee of equality under Article 16. The 2020 Code on Social Security defines “gig worker” and “platform worker” but excludes “contract labour” in government establishments, leaving women in outsourced sanitation, anganwadi and mid‑day‑meal schemes without maternity benefits, pension or occupational safety coverage. The 2022 amendment to the Contract Labour (Regulation and Abolition) Act, 1970, introduced a “principal employer” liability for social security contributions, yet state governments have not notified the rules for public‑sector contractors.

Survey evidence (PLFS) shows that women in contractual public‑sector jobs have far shorter average tenure than regular male employees, and very few are covered by employer‑provided health insurance. The National Commission for Women (NCW) has recommended: (i) extending the Maternity Benefit Act, 1961, to all contract workers in government-funded programmes; (ii) mandating a minimum quota for women in new regular recruitment drives in PSUs; and (iii) creating a “Public Sector Gender Equality Index” linked to disinvestment proceeds, so that a portion of sale revenue is earmarked for women’s skill‑upgradation and pension portability.

Answer‑Writing Technique: Comparative Policy Analysis
Question: “Critically examine how globalisation‑driven contractualisation in the public sector has affected women’s employment security. Suggest two institutional reforms.”
Structure the answer in three paragraphs. First, quantify the shift: cite official data (regular PSU workforce declined 1991‑2020, contract workforce expanded) and PLFS gender‑disaggregated tenure figures. Second, analyse mechanisms: (a) exclusion of contract workers from Maternity Benefit Act and Sixth Pay Commission parity; (b) judicial gap — Babita Puniya addressed only defence services; (c) Social Security Code 2020 omits government contract labour. Third, propose reforms grounded in existing instruments: (1) amend the Contract Labour Act rules to bring government contractors under principal‑employer liability for maternity and pension; (2) link a “Gender Equality Index” to disinvestment receipts under the 15th Finance Commission’s performance‑grant framework, ensuring fiscal incentive for regularising women’s posts. Conclude with a one‑sentence forward‑looking statement linking to SDG 8.5.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for “critical examination”, always open with a data‑backed trend (e.g., PLFS, DPE), then map the legal‑institutional gaps, and finish with reforms that reference a specific budgetary or legislative lever (Finance Commission window, Code amendment). This three‑step template scores high on structure and specificity.

Urban Governance and Ward‑Level Data in Targeting Multidimensional Poverty

The 74th Constitutional Amendment (1992) mandated Ward Committees for municipalities with populations above three lakh, but the functional devolution of data‑collection authority remains limited. The Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA) launched the “National Urban Data Framework” (NUDF) in 2021, prescribing indicators across multiple sectors, yet a low share of Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) report ward‑level data on the NUDF portal. Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) estimates for urban India (NITI Aayog, 2023) show a single-digit headcount ratio but mask sharp intra‑city variation: some Mumbai wards report MPI above 30 % while neighbouring wards are below 10 %. The absence of granular, gender‑disaggregated data prevents targeted interventions such as the Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana‑Urban (PMAY‑U) beneficiary selection, which still relies on the 2011 SECC.

The 2022 “Smart Cities Mission” revision introduced a “City Data Officer” (CDO) post in each of the 100 mission cities, tasked with integrating ward‑level administrative data (property tax, water connections, solid‑waste collection) with survey‑based deprivation metrics. Pilot projects in select cities demonstrated that linking property‑tax data with the National Food Security Act (NFSA) ration‑card database identified additional eligible households, a majority of which were female‑headed. However, the CDO role is contractual, lacks statutory backing, and faces resistance from state‑level Directorate of Municipal Administration which controls the Municipal Information System (MIS).

A viable institutional design is the “Ward‑Level Poverty Monitoring Cell” (WLPMC) anchored in the Ward Committee, with a mandated composition: (i) elected councillor (chair), (ii) CDO or equivalent data analyst, (iii) representative of the city‑level SHG federation, (iv) officer from the District Urban Development Agency (DUDA). The WLPMC would produce a quarterly “Ward Deprivation Dashboard” feeding into the Urban Health Index and the UCRI, thereby unlocking performance‑linked grants under the 15th Finance Commission’s “Urban Health and Nutrition” window. The 2023 Model Municipal Law (draft) circulated by MoHUA includes a clause for such cells, but adoption by state legislatures is pending.

Social Protection for Home‑Based Workers: Legal Gaps and Emerging Collective Bargaining Models

Home‑based workers (HBWs) — predominantly women engaged in garment stitching, beedi rolling, agarbatti making and electronics assembly — constitute an estimated tens of millions of workers in urban India (ILO‑WIEGO estimates). The Unorganised Workers’ Social Security Act, 2008, and the subsequent Code on Social Security, 2020, define “home‑based worker” but do not prescribe a dedicated contributory scheme; they are subsumed under the generic “unorganised worker” category, which entitles them only to the Pradhan Mantri Shram Yogi Maan‑Dhan (PM‑SYM) pension (₹3,000/month after 60) and the Ayushman Bharat health cover. Neither scheme addresses occupational hazards (repetitive strain, chemical exposure) or provides maternity cash benefits, because the Maternity Benefit Act, 1961, applies only to establishments with ten or more employees.

The 2019 Karnataka Home‑Based Workers (Conditions of Employment and Welfare) Bill, though not enacted, pioneered a tripartite registration board with employer, worker and state representation, and mandated a cess on contractor turnover to fund a welfare fund. Tamil Nadu’s 2021 “Home‑Based Workers Welfare Board” (established by executive order) registers workers and disburses a one‑time maternity assistance, but coverage remains low relative to estimated HBWs in the state. The Self‑Employed Women’s Association (SEWA) has piloted “piece‑rate agreements” with large apparel buyers, embedding a “social clause” that requires the buyer to contribute to a worker‑managed health fund. Early agreements cover several thousand workers and have shown reductions in out‑of‑pocket health expenditure (SEWA monitoring).

Scaling these models requires: (1) a central amendment to the Code on Social Security inserting a “Home‑Based Workers Welfare Fund” financed by a cess on the turnover of lead firms in global value chains sourcing from Indian HBWs; (2) mandatory registration of HBWs through the e‑Shram portal with a unique “HBW‑ID” linked to Aadhaar, enabling direct benefit transfer of maternity and sickness cash benefits; (3) recognition of HBW collectives as “trade unions” under the Trade Unions Act, 1926, to enable collective bargaining with contractors and brands. The 2024 ILO‑India technical cooperation programme is piloting a “Digital Piece‑Rate Transparency Platform” in Bengaluru that records piece rates, hours worked and payments in real time, providing evidence for wage‑floor enforcement.

Climate Adaptation Finance and Gender‑Responsive Budgeting in Peri‑Urban Areas

Peri‑urban zones — the transitional belts between municipal limits and rural hinterlands — house a significant share of India’s urban population (Census 2011) and absorb the bulk of climate‑induced migration. The 15th Finance Commission allocated substantial funds for “Urban Climate Resilience” (2021‑26), but the disbursement guidelines require cities to submit Climate Change Action Plans (CCAPs) with a “Gender Marker” aligned to OECD‑DAC methodology. Audits of CCAPs find that only a small fraction of adaptation budgets are tagged with a gender marker, and of those, a majority are limited to “awareness campaigns” rather than structural investments such as flood‑resilient housing or gender‑segregated sanitation in relocation sites.

The National Adaptation Fund for Climate Change (NAFCC) approved a project for “Gender‑Responsive Flood Resilience in Peri‑Urban Kolkata” (2022‑25). The project design includes: (i) community‑level women’s collectives managing early‑warning dissemination via digital groups; (ii) construction of elevated shelters with separate sanitation blocks for women and children; (iii) a revolving fund for women’s micro‑enterprises (fish‑drying, handicrafts) to restore livelihoods post‑flood. Mid‑term evaluation reports high access to early warnings, but shelter utilisation remains low because location planning did not incorporate women’s daily mobility routes to markets and schools.

To close the gap, the Ministry of Finance’s 2024 “Gender Budgeting Handbook for Urban Local Bodies” prescribes a three‑step process: (a) gender‑disaggregated vulnerability mapping at the ward level using the Urban Climate Resilience Index (UCRI) indicators; (b) earmarking a minimum share of CCAP adaptation outlay for women‑specific infrastructure, tracked through a “Gender‑Tagged Expenditure Ledger” integrated with the Public Financial Management System (PFMS); (c) performance‑based top‑up grants from the Finance Commission’s climate‑resilience window conditional on achieving a “Gender Marker Score” above a defined threshold. The handbook also recommends institutionalising a “Peri‑Urban Gender Climate Council” chaired by the District Collector, with representation from the ULB, the State Rural Livelihoods Mission, and women’s collectives, to oversee implementation and resolve land‑tenure disputes that disproportionately affect women in informal settlements.

  • Assuming that joint titling in town planning schemes automatically translates into women’s credit access without verifying bank‑level lending practices.
  • Treating the Social Security Code 2020 as a comprehensive cover for home‑based workers; the Code lacks a dedicated HBW fund and maternity cash benefit.
  • Equating the presence of a City Data Officer with functional ward‑level data systems; most CDOs are contractual and lack statutory authority.
  • Believing that gender‑marker tagging in CCAPs guarantees gender‑responsive outcomes; many tags are applied to soft activities rather than capital investment.
  • Overlooking the exclusion of government contract workers from the Maternity Benefit Act; the 2022 Contract Labour amendment has not been notified for public‑sector contractors.
  • Confusing the e‑Shram registration of home‑based workers with entitlement to employer‑linked benefits; e‑Shram is a database, not a contributory scheme.
  • Assuming peri‑urban climate projects automatically benefit women because they target “communities”; without gender‑segregated design, women’s mobility and sanitation needs remain unmet.

Gendered Dimensions of Urban Mobility Planning: Last‑Mile Connectivity, Non‑Motorised Transport and Safety Audits

Urban mobility policies in India have historically prioritised motorised corridors, leaving the “last‑mile” — the segment between a transit stop and the final destination — underserved. Women, who constitute a higher share of public‑transport users and undertake more trip chaining (combining work, care, and market trips), experience disproportionate time poverty when last‑mile options are unsafe, unaffordable, or absent. The National Urban Transport Policy and the Smart Cities Mission both recognise non‑motorised transport (NMT) as a core pillar, yet implementation remains fragmented. Cities such as Pune and Bengaluru have piloted “complete streets” with dedicated cycle tracks, footpaths, and street lighting, but coverage remains limited on arterial networks. Safety audits, encouraged under the Nirbhaya Fund framework, are conducted sporadically; audits in major metros have revealed widespread gaps in functional CCTV, adequate lighting, and designated women‑only waiting areas. The absence of gender‑disaggregated ridership data hampers evidence‑based design: most city mobility plans rely on aggregate peak‑hour counts, obscuring off‑peak travel patterns typical of care‑related trips. Integrating gender‑responsive budgeting into Comprehensive Mobility Plans (CMPs) — earmarking a dedicated share of NMT capital outlay for women‑centric infrastructure such as wide, well‑lit footpaths, pram‑friendly ramps, and secure bicycle parking — has shown promise in pilot areas to increase female cyclist participation. Institutionalising a “Gender Mobility Cell” within the Unified Metropolitan Transport Authority (UMTA), with representation from women’s collectives, the Department of Women and Child Development, and the traffic police, can ensure that safety audits feed directly into design revisions and that last‑mile services (e‑rickshaws, feeder buses, shared‑auto pools) are procured with gender‑sensitive service level agreements.

Impact of Digital Platform Labour on Women’s Time Use and Care Work: Evidence from Ride‑Hailing and Delivery Apps

The proliferation of app‑based ride‑hailing (Ola, Uber) and food‑delivery platforms (Swiggy, Zomato) has created a new segment of “platform workers” classified as independent contractors, excluding them from labour‑law protections such as minimum wages, maternity benefits, and occupational‑health coverage. Women constitute a significant minority of driver‑partners and delivery partners in major metros, yet platform algorithms allocate orders based on proximity and acceptance rates, penalising workers who decline trips due to care responsibilities or safety concerns. Studies indicate that female drivers spend substantially more time on unpaid care work than male counterparts, and a large share report turning down night‑shift orders because of inadequate street lighting and harassment risk. The algorithmic “surge pricing” model incentivises peak‑hour availability, clashing with school‑run and elder‑care schedules. Platforms have introduced “women‑only” ride options and “flex‑time” slots, but these are limited to a few cities and do not alter the underlying piece‑rate structure. The Code on Social Security (2020) defines “gig workers” and “platform workers” but leaves the design of a welfare fund to state governments; only a handful of states have notified draft rules, and most lack a maternity cash benefit. A viable policy route is to mandate “algorithmic transparency reports” — periodic disclosures of order‑allocation logic, rejection penalties, and earnings distribution by gender — coupled with a portable benefits account linked to the e‑Shram portal, where contributions are levied as a percentage of platform revenue. Collective bargaining initiatives demonstrate that when women’s collectives negotiate platform‑level “care‑leave” clauses, acceptance‑rate penalties decline and average daily earnings improve.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which of the following best explains why women platform workers experience higher time poverty than men?” Options include (a) lower vehicle ownership, (b) algorithmic penalties for rejecting night orders, (c) higher educational qualifications, (d) stronger union representation.
Correct answer: (b). Algorithmic penalties for rejecting night orders directly increase unpaid waiting time and force women to choose between care duties and earnings, whereas vehicle ownership, education, and union density are not primary drivers of the observed time‑poverty gap.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links digital platforms, gender, and labour law, first identify the regulatory gap (e.g., Code on Social Security 2020 lacks maternity cash benefit for gig workers). Then match the gap to the concrete outcome (time poverty, earnings penalty). This two‑step mapping avoids generic “women are vulnerable” answers.

Fiscal Federalism and Urban Poverty Alleviation: Role of State Finance Commissions and Centrally Sponsored Schemes

Urban local bodies (ULBs) in India derive a small share of their revenue from own sources; the bulk comes from state transfers and centrally sponsored schemes (CSS) such as the Deendayal Antyodaya Yojana‑National Urban Livelihoods Mission (DAY‑NULM) and the Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana‑Urban (PMAY‑U). The 74th Constitutional Amendment mandates State Finance Commissions (SFCs) to recommend the devolution of taxes, duties, and grants to ULBs every five years, yet many states have not constituted their SFCs on schedule. Where SFC recommendations are accepted, the vertical share for urban bodies remains well below the benchmarks suggested by successive Finance Commissions. Horizontal allocation formulas often weight population and area but ignore poverty concentration, resulting in significantly lower per‑capita transfers for ULBs with high slum densities. CSS guidelines require a state matching share, but many states delay releases, causing substantial project‑level fund gaps in slum‑rehabilitation components. The 15th Finance Commission introduced a performance‑based grant window tied to property‑tax collection efficiency and audited accounts; however, only a small fraction of eligible ULBs qualified in the initial tranche because of weak cadastral databases. A pragmatic reform is to embed a “poverty‑adjusted devolution index” in SFC formulas — combining multidimensional poverty headcount, slum household share, and informal‑worker density — and to link CSS release calendars to quarterly ULB financial‑management dashboards. Pilot efforts in some states have shown that such adjustments can increase per‑capita grants to high‑poverty municipalities and boost skill‑training enrolments under DAY‑NULM.

Climate Resilience and Women’s Land Rights in Coastal Urban Agglomerations: Legal Frameworks and Community Governance

Coastal megacities — Mumbai, Chennai, Kolkata, Visakhapatnam — house tens of millions, with informal settlements occupying low‑lying intertidal zones. Women in these settlements face a double jeopardy: insecure tenure (often no title, only “occupation certificates”) and heightened exposure to cyclones, storm surges, and sea‑level rise. The Coastal Regulation Zone (CRZ) Notification 2019, while easing some development restrictions, retains a “No Development Zone” (NDZ) along the high‑tide line, within which many slum dwellings fall. The Forest Rights Act (2006) does not extend to urban coastal commons, leaving women without statutory recognition of customary use rights over mangroves and tidal flats that they manage for fuelwood, fish drying, and waste recycling. The Maharashtra Slum Areas (Improvement, Clearance and Redevelopment) Act 1971 provides for “in‑situ rehabilitation” but requires a high consent threshold of “eligible slum dwellers”, a criterion that often excludes women whose names are absent from household surveys. Community‑led “Climate Resilience Committees” (CRCs) in several coastal settlements have demonstrated that when women hold substantial representation, relocation plans incorporate gender‑segregated sanitation, child‑care centres, and livelihood‑linked micro‑enterprise spaces. The National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) guidelines recommend “gender‑responsive relocation” but lack binding fiscal instruments. Embedding a “Women’s Land Rights Clause” in the CRZ framework — mandating joint titling of rehabilitation housing, recognition of women’s usufruct rights over coastal commons, and a dedicated “Coastal Women’s Resilience Fund” financed through a cess on port‑related revenues — would align legal tenure with climate adaptation outcomes.

Social Protection for Migrant Construction Workers: Portability, Occupational Health and the Building and Other Construction Workers Act

The Building and Other Construction Workers (Regulation of Employment and Conditions of Service) Act, 1996 (BOCW Act) and the accompanying Building and Other Construction Workers Welfare Cess Act, 1996, establish a cess of 1‑2 % on construction costs, collected by state governments and deposited in State Welfare Boards. Cumulative cess collections are substantial, yet a large portion remains undisbursed. Migrant workers, who constitute a majority of the construction workforce in metros, face three structural barriers: (1) registration requires a permanent address and employer‑issued identity card, which most inter‑state migrants lack; (2) benefits such as maternity assistance, disability pension, and scholarships for children are tied to the state of registration, rendering them non‑portable; (3) occupational‑health services — periodic medical examinations, silicosis screening, and first‑aid kits — are mandated only for larger establishments, excluding the vast majority of small‑contractor sites. The 2020 Code on Social Security subsumes the BOCW Act but retains the same registration architecture. Kerala’s “Aadhaar‑linked portable registration” pilot allowed workers to enroll via the e‑Shram portal, receive a unique “Construction Worker ID”, and access benefits across states; early results indicate increased maternity‑benefit claims and higher occupational‑health camp attendance. Scaling this model requires (a) a central amendment to the BOCW Cess Act mandating inter‑state portability of the welfare fund, (b) mandatory digital registration of all construction workers at entry points (railway stations, bus terminals) using biometric verification, and (c) a “Construction Health Card” linked to the Ayushman Bharat Digital Mission, enabling cashless treatment for work‑related injuries irrespective of employer. The 15th Finance Commission’s “Urban Health and Wellness Centres” allocation can be leveraged to site occupational‑health clinics near major construction clusters, with a stipulated female‑staff quota to address gender‑specific hazards such as reproductive‑health risks from heavy lifting and exposure to cement dust.

  • Assuming that the mere existence of a State Finance Commission guarantees equitable urban transfers; most SFCs use population‑area formulas that ignore poverty concentration.
  • Treating platform‑worker “flex‑time” options as a substitute for statutory maternity benefits; they do not provide cash compensation or job protection.
  • Believing that CRZ 2019 automatically secures women’s tenure in coastal slums; the notification lacks gender‑specific land‑rights provisions.
  • Confusing the BOCW cess collection rate with actual benefit disbursement; utilisation remains low nationally.
  • Equating the presence of a “Gender Mobility Cell” with functional safety audits; many cells lack budgetary authority and police coordination.
  • Overlooking the exclusion of home‑based workers from the Building and Other Construction Workers Act; they fall under a different, weaker regulatory regime.
  • Assuming that joint titling in rehabilitation housing automatically translates into credit access without verifying bank lending practices.

Urban Climate Resilience and Gendered Disaster Risk Reduction

India’s urban centres face compounding climate hazards — heatwaves, flash floods, cyclonic storm surges — that intersect with entrenched gender inequalities. The National Disaster Management Plan (NDMP) 2019 mandates gender‑responsive risk assessments, yet most city‑level Disaster Management Plans (DMPs) treat gender as a cross‑cutting theme rather than a design parameter. Studies of recent urban floods indicate that women in informal settlements experience significantly longer displacement durations, largely because evacuation shelters lack separate sanitation, child‑care spaces, and safe lighting. The Sendai Framework’s “Priority 4: Enhancing disaster preparedness” calls for sex‑disaggregated data; however, the Census 2011 and the Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS) do not capture intra‑household vulnerability, leaving planners without baseline metrics.

Mechanisms that have shown promise include: (1) “Gender‑Responsive Early Warning Systems” piloted in Odisha’s coastal municipalities, where women’s self‑help groups (SHGs) operate community radio alerts and manage evacuation registers; (2) “Urban Heat Action Plans” (UHAPs) adopted by cities such as Ahmedabad and Nagpur, which provide cool‑roof subsidies prioritising households headed by women and schedule water‑distribution points during peak heat hours; (3) the “Community‑Based Disaster Risk Management” (CBDRM) model under the National Urban Livelihoods Mission (NULM), where women’s collectives participate in designing flood‑resilient housing layouts and securing tenure through joint titles under the Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana (Urban).

Key gaps remain: (a) the Disaster Management Act, 2005 does not earmark a gender budget line; (b) State Disaster Response Funds (SDRF) are released post‑event, limiting pre‑emptive gender‑specific infrastructure; (c) the 15th Finance Commission’s “Urban Health and Wellness Centres” allocation can be leveraged for heat‑stress clinics, but few states have integrated gender‑segregated triage protocols. A binding “Gender‑Responsive Climate Adaptation Clause” in the Model Building Bye‑Laws, coupled with a dedicated “Women’s Resilience Fund” financed through a municipal green bond, would align fiscal instruments with the NDMP’s intent.

Social Protection for Platform Workers: Algorithmic Governance and Collective Bargaining

The rapid expansion of digital labour platforms — ride‑hailing, food‑delivery, freelance marketplaces — has created a workforce that falls outside the traditional employer‑employee nexus. The Code on Social Security, 2020 defines “platform worker” and mandates a Platform Workers Welfare Fund financed by a 1‑2 % cess on platform revenue, yet the operational rules for fund collection, benefit portability, and algorithmic transparency remain under‑developed. In practice, platforms classify workers as “partners”, denying them statutory minimum wages, maternity benefits, and occupational‑health coverage. A 2022 Supreme Court judgment recognised the right to collective bargaining for app‑based transport workers but stopped short of mandating algorithmic audit.

Three structural barriers dominate: (1) opaque algorithmic management — dynamic pricing, de‑activation thresholds, and rating systems function as de‑facto disciplinary tools without due process; (2) fragmented registration — workers must enroll separately on each platform and on the e‑Shram portal, leading to duplication and exclusion; (3) absence of portable benefits — accident insurance, pension contributions, and skill‑upgrading vouchers are tied to a single platform’s ecosystem. Kerala’s “Platform Workers Welfare Board” pilot introduced a unified digital identity linked to Aadhaar, enabling cross‑platform benefit portability and a grievance redressal mechanism that audits algorithmic changes periodically.

International comparators offer design lessons: the European Union’s “Platform Work Directive” (2024) obliges platforms to disclose key algorithmic parameters, provides a presumption of employment status, and establishes a sectoral social dialogue committee. Canada’s “Digital Platform Workers’ Bill of Rights” (2021) mandates transparent pay statements and a portable benefits account managed by a tripartite board. For India, a legislative amendment to the Code on Social Security should (a) embed an “Algorithmic Transparency Clause” requiring platforms to publish logic for work allocation, rating, and de‑activation; (b) create a “National Platform Workers’ Social Security Account” portable across platforms and states; (c) institutionalise a tripartite “Platform Labour Council” with representation from platform associations, worker collectives, and the Ministry of Labour, empowered to negotiate minimum earnings floors and health‑safety standards.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which of the following statements about the Code on Social Security, 2020 is correct? (a) It automatically classifies all platform workers as employees. (b) It establishes a Platform Workers Welfare Fund financed by a cess on platform revenue. (c) It mandates algorithmic audit by the Comptroller and Auditor General. (d) It provides for portable maternity benefits across all platforms.”
Correct answer: (b). The Code defines platform workers and creates a welfare fund financed by a 1‑2 % cess on platform revenue. It does not automatically confer employee status, does not assign audit to the CAG, and portable maternity benefits are not yet operationalised.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question mixes platform‑worker definitions with social‑security provisions, first identify the statutory source (Code on Social Security, 2020). Eliminate options that confer full employee status or assign audit functions to bodies not mentioned in the Act. Look for keywords “cess”, “welfare fund”, “portable benefits” — these are the operative mechanisms the examiner tests.

Intersectionality of Caste, Gender and Disability in Access to Urban Housing

Urban housing policy in India operates through multiple overlapping frameworks — the Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana (Urban), the Slum Rehabilitation Authority (SRA) schemes, and state‑level “Housing for All” missions. While each scheme includes reservation quotas for Scheduled Castes (SC), Scheduled Tribes (ST), and persons with disabilities (PwD), the interaction of these categories is rarely addressed. Census data indicates a significant share of urban households are headed by women with disabilities, a disproportionate number belonging to SC/ST communities. Yet the PMAY‑U guidelines treat gender, caste, and disability as separate eligibility filters, leading to “double exclusion” where a Dalit woman with a locomotor disability fails to meet any single quota threshold.

Legal precedents clarify the obligation: the Supreme Court has held that reasonable accommodation under the Rights of Persons with Disabilities Act, 2016 extends to housing allocation processes. The Madras High Court directed the Tamil Nadu Slum Clearance Board to adopt an “intersectional vulnerability index” that weights caste, gender, disability, and age for in‑situ rehabilitation priority. However, most municipal corporations lack the data infrastructure to compute such an index; the Socio‑Economic and Caste Census (SECC) 2011 does not capture disability status, and the Unique Disability ID (UDID) database is not linked to housing MIS.

Operational reforms that have shown promise: (1) the “Unified Housing Eligibility Portal” piloted in Pune, which merges SECC, UDID, and Aadhaar data to auto‑calculate a composite vulnerability score; (2) the “Accessibility‑First Design Mandate” in the Model Building Bye‑Laws (2023 amendment) requiring ramp access, tactile flooring, and visual alarms in all new low‑income housing; (3) the “Community Land Trust” model in Bhubaneswar, where women’s collectives from SC/ST backgrounds hold collective title, enabling them to negotiate design modifications for disability access. Scaling these requires a central directive to integrate UDID with the PMAY‑U MIS, a mandatory “Intersectional Impact Assessment” for every housing project above 500 units, and earmarked budget lines under the 15th Finance Commission’s “Urban Housing” grant for accessibility retrofits.

Impact of Global Value Chains on Women's Informal Home‑Based Work

Global value chains (GVCs) in textiles, electronics assembly, and agro‑processing increasingly outsource labour‑intensive stages to home‑based workers in Indian cities. The International Labour Organization (ILO) estimates that millions of women in India perform home‑based work linked to export‑oriented supply chains, yet they remain invisible in official labour statistics because the Periodic Labour Force Survey classifies them as “self‑employed”. The 2019 amendment to the Contract Labour (Regulation and Abolition) Act does not cover home‑based workers, and the proposed “Home‑Based Workers (Social Security) Bill” has lapsed multiple times.

Mechanisms of exploitation are structural: (1) piece‑rate pricing set by lead firms through tier‑1 suppliers, leaving no room for negotiation; (2) lack of occupational‑health safeguards — exposure to chemical dyes, soldering fumes, and repetitive‑strain injuries without workers’ compensation; (3) absence of collective bargaining channels — home‑based workers are geographically dispersed and not recognised as a “workforce” under the Industrial Relations Code, 2020. The “Self‑Employed Women’s Association” (SEWA) model in Gujarat demonstrates that organising home‑based workers into cooperatives can secure bulk‑purchase of raw materials, negotiate minimum piece rates, and access the “Mahatma Gandhi Bunkar Bima Yojana” for health insurance.

Policy levers to embed decent work in GVCs include: (a) a mandatory “Home‑Based Worker Clause” in the Foreign Trade Policy’s export‑promotion schemes, requiring lead firms to disclose sub‑contracting chains and comply with the ILO Home Work Convention (C177), which India has not ratified but can adopt voluntarily; (b) extending the “Social Security Code, 2020” definition of “worker” to include home‑based workers, enabling portable provident fund and maternity benefits; (c) establishing a “National Home‑Based Workers’ Registry” linked to the e‑Shram portal, with a grievance redressal mechanism for wage theft and unsafe conditions. The 15th Finance Commission’s “Urban Livelihoods” grant can fund community‑level “Workplace Safety Kits” and digital skill‑upgrading centres managed by women’s collectives.

Fiscal Decentralisation and Gender‑Responsive Urban Service Delivery

The 74th Constitutional Amendment envisages empowered Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) with devolved functions, finances, and functionaries. In practice, fiscal decentralisation remains asymmetric: property‑tax collection averages well below the benchmark set by the 15th Finance Commission. State Finance Commissions (SFCs) often rely on population‑area formulas that ignore poverty concentration, resulting in per‑capita transfers that disadvantage municipalities with large informal settlements. Gender‑responsive budgeting (GRB) at the municipal level is mandated by the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA) circular (2021), yet a small minority of ULBs publish a gender‑budget statement.

Key structural constraints: (1) limited own‑source revenue — ULBs depend heavily on state grants, reducing autonomy to prioritise gender‑specific services such as safe‑city lighting, crèches, and menstrual‑health facilities; (2) parallel centrally sponsored schemes (CSS) — Swachh Bharat Mission (Urban), Atal Mission for Rejuvenation and Urban Transformation (AMRUT), and Smart Cities Mission — bypass ULB budgets, creating fragmented accountability; (3) weak capacity — municipal cadres lack training in gender‑disaggregated data analysis and performance‑based budgeting.

Reform roadmap: (a) amend the Municipal Corporation Acts to mandate a minimum share of own‑source revenue earmarked for gender‑responsive capital works, audited by the State Audit Department; (b) redesign SFC transfer formulas to incorporate a “Multidimensional Poverty Index” weight, ensuring higher per‑capita flows to wards with high female‑headed household density; (c) integrate CSS funds into a single “Urban Service Delivery Window” at the ULB level, with a gender‑tagging module in the Public Financial Management System (PFMS); (d) institutionalise “Ward‑Level Gender Audits” conducted by women’s SHGs, feeding results into the annual budget cycle. The 15th Finance Commission’s “Performance‑Based Grants” for ULBs can be linked to measurable improvements in gender‑disaggregated service indicators — water‑collection time, safe‑transport access, and maternal‑health outreach coverage.

  • Assuming that the mere existence of a gender‑budget line guarantees expenditure on women‑specific services; many ULBs re‑allocate funds mid‑year without council approval.
  • Treating platform‑worker “flex‑time” options as a substitute for statutory maternity benefits; they do not provide cash compensation or job protection.
  • Believing that the 74th Amendment automatically ensures fiscal autonomy; most states have not devolved the 18 functions listed in the Twelfth Schedule.
  • Confusing the BOCW cess collection rate with actual benefit disbursement; utilisation remains low nationally.
  • Equating the presence of a “Gender Mobility Cell” with functional safety audits; many cells lack budgetary authority and police coordination.
  • Overlooking the exclusion of home‑based workers from the Building and Other Construction Workers Act; they fall under a different, weaker regulatory regime.
  • Review these traps before the exam to avoid common errors.

Gendered Impact of Urban Renewal and Slum Rehabilitation Schemes on Women's Livelihoods

Urban renewal programmes such as the Jawaharlal Nehru National Urban Renewal Mission (JNNURM), the Rajiv Awas Yojana (RAY) and the Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana‑Urban (PMAY‑U) have reshaped the spatial layout of Indian cities. While the stated objective is to provide pucca housing with basic services, the implementation pathway often disrupts the informal economic activities that women depend on. Home‑based work — garment stitching, agarbatti rolling, food processing — is typically located within the dwelling unit. Relocation to peripheral resettlement colonies increases travel time to markets, raises transport costs and severs social networks that facilitate piece‑rate contracts. Research by institutions such as the National Institute of Urban Affairs indicates that women in resettlement sites frequently report significant earnings declines in the initial years after displacement.

Titling practices further embed gender bias. In many states the allotment letter is issued in the name of the male head of household, leaving women without legal proof of residence. This undermines their ability to access credit, enroll children in nearby schools, or claim entitlements under the National Food Security Act. The Supreme Court’s judgment in Olga Tellis v. Bombay Municipal Corporation (1985) recognised the right to livelihood as part of Article 21, yet rehabilitation schemes rarely conduct gender‑disaggregated livelihood impact assessments before demolition. The Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs’ guidelines for in‑situ slum rehabilitation call for a “livelihood restoration plan”, but compliance is monitored largely through self‑reporting by implementing agencies.

Participatory mechanisms such as Slum Dwellers’ Federations and women’s Self‑Help Groups (SHGs) have negotiated better outcomes where they are formally recognised. In Pune, women’s collectives have co‑managed the design of common facilities — childcare centres, solid‑waste segregation points, and safe‑water kiosks — ensuring that the rebuilt environment supports reproductive and productive work. Scaling such models requires amending State Slum Rehabilitation Acts to make gender‑responsive livelihood audits a statutory precondition for project approval.

Urban Land Value Capture Mechanisms and Gender Equity in Tenure Security

Land value capture (LVC) instruments — betterment levy, transfer of development rights (TDR), land pooling, and premium floor‑space index (FSI) — are increasingly used to finance urban infrastructure. The 15th Finance Commission highlighted LVC as a key source of own‑source revenue for Urban Local Bodies (ULBs). However, the distributional consequences of these tools are rarely analysed through a gender lens. In Mumbai’s Development Plan 2034, TDR generated from slum rehabilitation projects is traded in a market dominated by large developers; the revenue accrues to the municipal corporation but is not earmarked for services that reduce women’s time poverty, such as community crèches or last‑mile public transport.

Betterment levy collections, mandated under the Maharashtra Regional and Town Planning Act, 1966, are often deposited in a general development fund. Audits by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India have found that only a small fraction of levy proceeds in the Mumbai Metropolitan Region were directed toward gender‑specific infrastructure. Land pooling schemes under Gujarat’s Town Planning and Urban Development Act, 1976, allocate reconstituted plots to original landowners, who are predominantly male. Women who cultivate or reside on pooled land but lack formal title are excluded from the benefit‑sharing formula, reinforcing tenure insecurity.

Gender‑responsive LVC design can be advanced through three policy levers: (a) statutory earmarking of a fixed share of LVC receipts for a “Women’s Urban Services Fund” with transparent utilisation reports; (b) mandatory joint titling of reconstituted plots or rehabilitation units in the names of both spouses, as piloted in Odisha’s “Jaga Mission”; (c) inclusion of women’s SHG representatives in the LVC governance committee that approves project‑level expenditure. The 74th Constitutional Amendment’s Twelfth Schedule lists “urban planning including town planning” as a municipal function; integrating gender budgeting into the LVC framework operationalises this mandate.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which of the following best describes the gender impact of Transfer of Development Rights (TDR) in Mumbai’s slum rehabilitation?” Options: (a) TDR automatically guarantees joint titles for women; (b) TDR revenue is ring‑fenced for women’s hostels; (c) TDR is traded in a developer‑dominated market and rarely funds gender‑specific services; (d) TDR eliminates the need for betterment levy.
Correct answer: (c). TDR in Mumbai is a marketable commodity; the proceeds flow into the general municipal fund without a statutory gender tag. Options (a) and (b) describe aspirational policies not yet enacted, while (d) confuses two distinct LVC instruments.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links land finance to gender outcomes, look for explicit earmarking language (“shall be utilised for”, “reserved for”). Absence of such language means the revenue is fungible and likely not reaching women‑targeted services.

Social Protection for Urban Waste Pickers: Legal Gaps, Collective Action and Emerging Models

Urban waste pickers — estimated at 1.5‑2 million nationwide — form the backbone of municipal solid‑waste recycling. The Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016 recognise “informal waste collectors” and mandate their integration into the formal waste‑management chain, yet most cities lack a regulatory framework to convert this recognition into entitlements. Waste pickers are excluded from the Building and Other Construction Workers (BOCW) Act, the Unorganised Workers’ Social Security Act, 2008, and the Social Security Code, 2020 because they are not classified as “workers” under any of these statutes. Consequently, they have no access to provident fund, health insurance, maternity benefits, or occupational injury compensation.

Collective action has filled part of the void. The Kagad Kach Patra Kashtakari Panchayat (KKPKP) in Pune, a union of waste pickers, negotiated a memorandum of understanding with the Pune Municipal Corporation that provides identity cards, access to municipal health centres, and a monthly stipend for sorting at designated material‑recovery facilities. In Bengaluru, the Hasiru Dala cooperative secured a contract to manage dry‑waste collection for several wards, enabling members to earn a minimum wage and contribute to a group‑managed health fund. These models demonstrate that formal recognition as “service providers” rather than “beneficiaries” unlocks labour‑rights protections.

Policy levers to scale such gains include: (a) amending the Social Security Code to define “waste picker” as a distinct category of gig‑worker, enabling portable benefits; (b) mandating ULBs to allocate a minimum share of their solid‑waste management budget to waste‑picker welfare funds, monitored through the Swachh Bharat Mission‑Urban dashboard; (c) extending the Employees’ State Insurance Corporation (ESIC) coverage to waste pickers through a “deemed employer” provision where the municipal corporation is treated as the principal employer for contracted sorting centres. The 15th Finance Commission’s “Urban Health and Sanitation” grant can be conditional on demonstrable inclusion of waste pickers in social‑protection registers.

Intersectionality of Caste, Gender and Migration in Urban Informal Settlements

Urban informal settlements are not homogeneous; they are stratified by caste, gender, and migration status. Dalit and Adivasi women who migrate from rural districts to cities such as Delhi, Surat, or Hyderabad occupy the most precarious niches — casual domestic work, construction head‑loading, and sanitation labour. The 2011 Census shows that Scheduled Caste (SC) households constitute a disproportionate share of slum populations in metropolitan areas, yet city‑level data rarely disaggregates poverty indicators by caste‑gender‑migration triads. This statistical invisibility translates into policy blind spots: water‑tap allocation, community‑toilet placement, and Anganwadi centre locations are planned on aggregate population norms, ignoring the safety concerns of Dalit women who face caste‑based harassment at shared facilities.

Labour‑market segmentation reinforces vulnerability. SC women migrants are over‑represented in “dirty, dangerous, and demeaning” jobs with no written contracts, making them ineligible for the Inter‑State Migrant Workmen Act, 1979, which applies only to establishments employing five or more migrant workers. The 2020 Code on Social Security extends coverage to “platform workers” and “gig workers” but retains the threshold‑based definition that excludes most home‑based and street‑level occupations dominated by Dalit women. Moreover, the Scheduled Castes and the Scheduled Tribes (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989, does not explicitly address economic exploitation in urban informal labour markets, leaving a legal vacuum.

Intersectional policy design requires: (a) mandatory caste‑gender‑migration disaggregation in the Socio‑Economic and Caste Census (SECC) and in ULB‑level ward surveys; (b) targeted “Dalit Women’s Urban Livelihood Missions” that combine skill‑upgrading, credit linkages through the National Scheduled Castes Finance and Development Corporation, and safe‑space community centres; (c) inclusion of caste‑based discrimination indicators in the monitoring framework of the Deendayal Antyodaya Yojana‑National Urban Livelihoods Mission (DAY‑NULM). The 2021 NITI Aayog “Multidimensional Poverty Index” report recommends such disaggregation but implementation remains at pilot stage in a few states.

Digital Governance and Women's Access to E‑Government Services: Gaps and Reform Pathways

The Digital India programme has rolled out platforms such as UMANG, DigiLocker, e‑District, and the National Scholarship Portal to deliver citizen‑centric services. While these platforms reduce transaction costs for the digitally literate, they deepen exclusion for women in low‑income urban households who face a “triple barrier”: device ownership, functional literacy, and language accessibility. The National Family Health Survey (NFHS‑5) indicates that a minority of urban women in the lowest wealth quintile own a mobile phone, and an even smaller proportion can independently navigate an app‑based service. Moreover, most e‑government interfaces are available only in English and the official state language, marginalising migrant women whose mother tongue differs.

Algorithmic decision‑making in welfare targeting — for example, the Aadhaar‑enabled Public Distribution System (PDS) authentication — introduces exclusion errors that disproportionately affect women. Biometric failures due to manual labour‑induced fingerprint wear, coupled with the lack of a fallback “iris‑only” or “OTP‑only” option, lead to denial of rations. The Supreme Court’s 2018 judgment in Justice K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India upheld Aadhaar but mandated alternative authentication mechanisms; compliance at the last‑mile remains patchy. Privacy concerns also deter women from registering on platforms that require sharing of location data, especially in contexts of domestic violence where digital footprints can be monitored by perpetrators.

Reform pathways must embed gender‑responsive design principles: (a) adopt a “mobile‑first, voice‑first” architecture with multilingual interactive voice response (IVR) for all citizen services; (b) mandate “gender‑impact assessments” for every new e‑government module, published on the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY) portal; (c) create a “Women’s Digital Assistance Corps” of community‑level facilitators — drawn from SHGs — trained to assist with onboarding, grievance redressal, and digital literacy; (d) integrate a “privacy‑by‑default” framework that allows users to limit data sharing to the minimum required for service delivery. The 15th Finance Commission’s digital infrastructure allocations can be tied to measurable improvements in women’s e‑service uptake rates, tracked through sex‑disaggregated analytics dashboards.

  • Assuming that land value capture revenue automatically benefits women because the municipality is “pro‑poor”.
  • Confusing the recognition of waste pickers in the Solid Waste Management Rules with statutory social‑security coverage.
  • Treating caste‑gender‑migration intersectionality as a “cross‑cutting theme” without demanding disaggregated data in scheme guidelines.
  • Believing that the mere existence of a multilingual e‑government portal guarantees access for illiterate women.
  • Equating the issuance of identity cards to waste pickers with full labour‑rights protection under the Social Security Code.
  • Overlooking the requirement for joint titling in rehabilitation schemes; single‑name titles perpetuate women’s tenure insecurity.
  • Recognising these traps helps avoid common misinterpretations in the exam.

Gendered Impact of Urban Water Pricing and Subsidy Design on Low‑Income Women

Urban water pricing reforms introduced under the Atal Mission for Rejuvenation and Urban Transformation (AMRUT) and the Jal Jeevan Mission (Urban) aim to recover operation and maintenance costs through volumetric tariffs. While cost‑recovery improves utility finances, the design of tariff blocks and subsidy targeting often overlooks the gendered division of labour in water collection and management. In most low‑income settlements, women bear primary responsibility for fetching, storing, and rationing water for drinking, cooking, sanitation, and home‑based enterprises such as food vending or textile work. When tariffs rise without a lifeline block that guarantees a minimum free or heavily subsidised volume, women are forced to reduce water use for hygiene, increasing the incidence of water‑borne diseases and menstrual health complications.

Subsidy mechanisms that rely on household‑level means testing — typically using the Socio‑Economic and Caste Census (SECC) or property tax records — frequently exclude women‑headed households that lack formal property titles or are not captured as separate consumption units. Draft National Water Policy revisions have recommended a “pro‑poor” tariff structure with a rising block design, but implementation varies across states. In cities where the lifeline block is set at typically 50–70 litres per capita per day, women in informal settlements often exceed this threshold due to shared standpipes and leakage losses, pushing them into higher tariff brackets. Moreover, the absence of gender‑disaggregated consumption data in utility billing systems prevents targeted cross‑subsidies.

Participatory tariff‑setting forums mandated by the 74th Constitutional Amendment and the Model Municipal Law have been instituted in a few municipal corporations, yet women’s representation remains tokenistic. Evidence from pilot projects supported by the Water Supply and Sanitation Collaborative Council (WSSCC) in states such as Odisha and Madhya Pradesh shows that when women’s self‑help groups (SHGs) are embedded in ward‑level water user committees, leakage reporting improves and lifeline block adherence rises. Legal backing for such participation can be drawn from the Right to Information Act, 2005, which enables citizens to demand transparency in tariff revision minutes, and from Supreme Court jurisprudence affirming the right to affordable water as part of the right to life under Article 21.

Policy levers to make water pricing gender‑responsive include: (a) a universal lifeline block of at least 70 litres per capita per day funded through a dedicated “Water Equity Fund” financed by a surcharge on commercial and industrial bulk users; (b) mandatory gender‑impact assessment of any tariff revision, published on the urban local body (ULB) website; (c) integration of SHG‑managed community water kiosks with smart metering that allows prepaid lifeline quotas; (d) disaggregated billing data by gender of the primary bill payer, collected during the next SECC round. These measures align with the Sustainable Development Goal 6.1 target of universal and equitable access to safe and affordable drinking water.

Public‑Private Partnerships in Urban Service Delivery: Gender Equity Outcomes and Accountability Gaps

Public‑Private Partnerships (PPPs) have become the dominant modality for financing and operating urban infrastructure — water supply, solid waste management, public transport, and street lighting — under the Smart Cities Mission and the National Infrastructure Pipeline. The standard concession agreements allocate revenue risk to private operators while the public authority retains regulatory oversight. However, the gender equity implications of these contracts are rarely articulated in the bidding documents or performance indicators. Women, who constitute the majority of users of public toilets, community water points, and last‑mile transport, experience service quality differently from men. For instance, PPP‑operated community toilets often lack separate female cubicles, adequate lighting, and menstrual waste disposal facilities, leading to under‑utilisation and safety concerns.

Accountability mechanisms in PPP contracts typically focus on financial metrics — availability payments, key performance indicators (KPIs) such as non‑revenue water reduction, or on‑time bus arrivals. Social KPIs, if present, are limited to generic “customer satisfaction” surveys that do not disaggregate responses by gender, caste, or disability. The 2018 Model Concession Agreement for Urban Water Supply issued by the Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs includes a clause on “pro‑poor service standards” but does not define gender‑specific benchmarks. Consequently, private operators have little incentive to invest in gender‑responsive design features such as women‑only waiting areas in bus rapid transit corridors or childcare facilities at waste‑to‑energy plants.

A Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) report on PPPs in urban water supply noted instances where private concessionaires failed to meet the stipulated “minimum service level” for slum clusters, and ULBs did not enforce penalties because contracts lacked clear dispute‑resolution timelines. Civil society audits in cities like Pune and Bhubaneswar have demonstrated that when women’s collectives were given a formal role in joint monitoring committees, service complaints dropped significantly and operators installed gender‑segregated sanitation blocks within months.

To close the accountability gap, the following reforms are recommended: (a) embed gender‑responsive KPIs — such as percentage of female‑headed households with functional household tap connections, availability of menstrual hygiene management facilities in public toilets, and safety audit scores for women’s last‑mile transport — into every PPP concession agreement; (b) mandate a “Gender Equity Audit” by an independent third party at mid‑term and project close‑out, with findings tabled in the municipal corporation’s standing committee; (c) allocate a fixed percentage of the concessionaire’s performance bonus to a “Women’s Service Improvement Fund” managed by ward‑level SHG federations; (d) amend the Public Procurement (Preference to Make in India) Order, 2017, to require bidders to submit a Gender Action Plan as part of the technical proposal. These steps would operationalise the constitutional mandate of Article 15(3) and the Directive Principle under Article 39(a) within the PPP framework.

Examiner's Trap
A question asks: “Which of the following is a common reason for the failure of gender‑responsive service delivery in urban PPP projects?” Options: (1) Private operators are legally prohibited from collecting gender‑disaggregated data. (2) Concession agreements lack gender‑specific performance indicators and monitoring mechanisms. (3) The 74th Amendment bars ULBs from entering PPPs for social infrastructure. (4) Women’s SHGs are not allowed to participate in joint monitoring committees.
Correct answer: (2). PPP concession agreements in India typically specify financial and technical KPIs but omit gender‑specific benchmarks. The 74th Amendment enables ULBs to enter PPPs; there is no legal bar on gender‑disaggregated data collection; and SHGs can be included in monitoring committees if the contract provides for it. The trap is to assume a legal prohibition where the gap is contractual and institutional.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question on PPPs and gender appears, first identify whether the stem refers to contractual design (concession agreement), regulatory oversight (ULB/CAG), or participatory governance (ward committees). The most frequent correct answer highlights the absence of gender‑specific KPIs in the contract. Memorise the phrase “gender‑responsive KPIs in concession agreements” as a keyword for quick recall.

Globalisation, Export‑Oriented Agro‑Processing and Women's Labour Conditions in Peri‑Urban Zones

The liberalisation of agricultural trade under the World Trade Organization’s Agreement on Agriculture and subsequent bilateral free‑trade agreements has spurred the growth of export‑oriented agro‑processing clusters in peri‑urban corridors — notably grape and pomegranate packhouses in Maharashtra’s Nashik district, mango pulp units in Andhra Pradesh’s Chittoor, and shrimp processing plants along the Tamil Nadu coast. These clusters rely heavily on a flexible, low‑cost female workforce for sorting, grading, peeling, and packaging. Women are recruited through labour contractors on piece‑rate contracts, often without written appointments, social security coverage, or occupational health safeguards.

The seasonal nature of horticultural exports creates a “peak‑season” surge in demand for female labour, leading to 12‑hour shifts, night work, and exposure to pesticide residues, cold‑storage environments, and repetitive‑strain injuries. The Factories Act, 1948, and the Occupational Safety, Health and Working Conditions Code, 2020, mandate crèche facilities, protective equipment, and limits on night work for women, but enforcement in peri‑urban units is weak because many establishments fall below the statutory employee threshold or operate under the “contract labour” exemption. An International Labour Organization (ILO) study on global value chains in Indian horticulture documented very low ESI coverage among female packhouse workers.

Global buyers increasingly impose social compliance standards — such as the Global Social Compliance Programme (GSCP) and Sedex Members Ethical Trade Audit (SMETA) — which require traceability of labour conditions down to the farm‑gate. While these private standards have prompted some lead firms to invest in worker welfare programmes, they remain voluntary and audit‑driven, often resulting in “paper compliance” rather than structural change. The 2021 amendment to the Foreign Trade Policy introduced the “Remission of Duties and Taxes on Exported Products” (RoDTEP) scheme, which incentivises export competitiveness but does not condition benefits on labour‑rights adherence.

Policy pathways to improve women’s labour conditions in these zones include: (a) extending the coverage of the Code on Social Security, 2020, to all contract and piece‑rate workers in agro‑processing irrespective of establishment size; (b) mandating gender‑disaggregated reporting in the Annual Survey of Industries for the food‑processing sector; (c) linking RoDTEP and Production‑Linked Incentive (PLI) scheme disbursements to verified compliance with the Occupational Safety, Health and Working Conditions Code; (d) supporting women’s collectives to negotiate collective bargaining agreements with lead firms, drawing on the model of the Self‑Employed Women’s Association (SEWA) in the Gujarat horticulture belt. These measures would align export competitiveness with the constitutional guarantee of just and humane conditions of work under Article 42.

Urban Informal Sector Formalisation Pathways: Women's Access to Credit, Skill Certification and Social Security

The urban informal economy — comprising home‑based workers, street vendors, domestic workers, and waste pickers — employs an estimated 80 % of the non‑agricultural female workforce. Formalisation efforts have traditionally focused on registration under the Unorganised Workers’ Social Security Act, 2008, and the subsequent Code on Social Security, 2020, which created a National Social Security Board and state‑level boards. However, registration rates among women remain low because the process requires proof of employment relationship, which is absent for self‑employed home‑based workers and for those engaged through multiple intermediaries.

Credit access is a critical lever for formalisation. The Pradhan Mantri Mudra Yojana (PMMY) and the Stand‑Up India scheme provide collateral‑free loans to micro‑enterprises, yet women’s uptake is constrained by the requirement of a business plan and a bankable credit history. The Self‑Help Group‑Bank Linkage Programme (SHG‑BLP) has demonstrated higher repayment rates when loans are channelled through women’s collectives, but the programme’s reach in urban areas is limited by the rural‑centric design of the National Rural Livelihoods Mission (NRLM). The 2022 revision of the Deendayal Antyodaya Yojana‑National Urban Livelihoods Mission (DAY‑NULM) introduced an “Urban SHG” component, but the allocation for credit linkage remains a fraction of the rural counterpart.

Skill certification under the Recognition of Prior Learning (RPL) component of the Pradhan Mantri Kaushal Vikas Yojana (PMKVY) offers a pathway to formal recognition of informal competencies — such as tailoring, food processing, and beauty services. Yet the RPL assessment centres are predominantly located in industrial training institutes, making them inaccessible to women with care responsibilities. The 2020 National Education Policy’s emphasis on “vocational integration” in school curricula has not yet translated into flexible, community‑based certification for adult women workers.

An integrated formalisation framework should combine: (a) a universal “Urban Informal Worker Identity Card” issued by the ULB, linked to the Aadhaar‑enabled e‑Shram portal, which serves as a single window for social security enrolment, skill certification, and credit referral; (b) a “Women’s Micro‑Enterprise Guarantee Fund” capitalised by the National Credit Guarantee Trustee Company, providing portfolio guarantees to banks lending to women’s SHG‑federated enterprises; (c) mobile RPL assessment units that operate in community centres and markets, with female assessors and childcare support; (d) mandatory inclusion of gender‑disaggregated outcomes in the monitoring dashboard of the Code on Social Security, reported quarterly to Parliament. Such a framework would operationalise the Directive Principle of Article 41 (right to work) and the ILO Convention 189 on Domestic Workers, which India has signed but not ratified.

Smart City Surveillance Infrastructure and Women's Right to Privacy, Mobility and Safety

The Smart Cities Mission has deployed Integrated Command and Control Centres (ICCCs) in over 100 cities, integrating CCTV networks, automatic number‑plate recognition, facial‑recognition analytics, and environmental sensors. While the stated objectives include traffic management, disaster response, and crime prevention, the pervasive surveillance architecture raises distinct concerns for women’s privacy, mobility, and safety. Women in informal settlements and low‑income neighbourhoods are disproportionately monitored because camera density is highest in “high‑crime” zones identified by police heat‑maps, which often correlate with poverty rather than actual offence rates.

Facial‑recognition systems trained on datasets that under‑represent women, especially those from marginalised castes and tribal groups, exhibit higher false‑positive rates for these populations. Reports by digital rights organisations such as the Internet Freedom Foundation have documented cases where women activists and street vendors were flagged by automated alerts during peaceful assemblies, leading to preventive detention under Section 144 of the Code of Criminal Procedure. Moreover, the lack of a statutory data‑protection framework — the Personal Data Protection Bill, 2019, lapsed and the Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023, is yet to be fully operationalised — means that video footage and biometric data can be retained indefinitely and shared across agencies without consent.

The Supreme Court’s 2017 judgment in Justice K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India recognised privacy as a fundamental right under Article 21 and laid down the proportionality test for state surveillance. Applying this test, any smart‑city surveillance measure must be (i) backed by law, (ii) pursue a legitimate aim, (iii) be necessary and proportionate, and (iv) include procedural safeguards such as independent oversight and data‑minimisation. Currently, most ICCCs operate under executive orders or municipal resolutions, not parliamentary legislation, and there is no independent oversight board with gender‑balanced representation.

Reform measures to align smart‑city surveillance with women’s rights include: (a) enacting a city‑level “Surveillance Governance Ordinance” that mandates purpose‑limitation, storage‑limitation, and mandatory deletion of non‑relevant footage within 30 days; (b) establishing a Gender‑Responsive Surveillance Audit Committee comprising women’s rights organisations, technical experts, and ULB officials, empowered to review camera placement, algorithmic bias assessments, and access logs; (c) integrating “safe‑route” mapping in the city’s open data portal, co‑created with women’s collectives, to guide mobility planning without relying on pervasive tracking; (d) allocating a portion of the Smart Cities Mission’s “Citizen Engagement” budget to digital literacy workshops that enable women to exercise their rights under the forthcoming data‑protection regime. These steps would ensure that technological upgrades enhance, rather than erode, the constitutional guarantees of dignity, equality, and freedom of movement for urban women.

  • Assuming that a universal lifeline water block automatically reaches women‑headed households without gender‑disaggregated metering.
  • Treating PPP “pro‑poor” clauses as sufficient for gender equity without mandatory gender‑specific KPIs and independent audits.
  • Believing that export‑oriented agro‑processing compliance with private social audits guarantees statutory labour‑rights coverage for female piece‑rate workers.
  • Equating registration on the e‑Shram portal with full social‑security entitlement for home‑based women workers who lack proof of employment relationship.
  • Assuming that smart‑city CCTV networks improve women’s safety simply because camera density is high, ignoring algorithmic bias and lack of legal oversight.
  • Confusing the existence of a Skill India RPL certificate with automatic access to formal credit and social security for urban informal women.
  • Overlooking the requirement for gender‑balanced independent oversight boards in surveillance governance, treating executive orders as sufficient legal basis.

Gendered Impact of Urban Flooding on Women's Informal Enterprises

Urban flooding disproportionately disrupts the livelihoods of women who operate home‑based and street‑level enterprises. The 2015 Chennai floods and the 2022 Bengaluru flooding highlighted how water‑logging destroys inventory, damages equipment, and forces prolonged closures of micro‑enterprises such as tailoring units, food‑processing stalls, and handicraft workshops predominantly run by women. Unlike formal firms, these enterprises lack insurance coverage, access to formal credit for reconstruction, and legal recognition to claim compensation under the Disaster Management Act, 2005. The National Urban Livelihoods Mission (NULM) provides a “Shelter for Urban Homeless” component but does not extend targeted flood‑recovery grants to women‑owned informal units. Municipal corporations typically rely on the State Disaster Response Fund (SDRF) for relief, yet the fund’s guidelines prioritize housing reconstruction over livelihood restoration. Consequently, women experience a double burden: loss of income and increased care responsibilities as they manage household recovery while navigating bureaucratic relief channels. Research indicates that women’s time‑use surveys after flood events show a significant increase in unpaid care work, reducing the hours available for enterprise revival. Policy responses that integrate gender‑disaggregated damage assessments into the Post‑Disaster Needs Assessment (PDNA) framework, and that earmark a portion of the SDRF for women‑led micro‑enterprise restart kits, would address this structural gap.

Urban Informal Housing and Women's Access to Affordable Housing Schemes

Access to secure, affordable housing remains a critical determinant of women’s economic stability in Indian cities. The Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana – Urban (PMAY‑U), launched in 2015, aims to provide “Housing for All” by 2022 (extended to 2024), yet its implementation reveals gendered exclusion patterns. The scheme’s “Beneficiary‑Led Construction” (BLC) vertical requires land ownership or a valid lease deed, a condition that many women in informal settlements cannot meet because property titles are traditionally held by male relatives. Census data indicates that a low percentage of urban households headed by women own the dwelling they occupy. Moreover, the Credit‑Linked Subsidy Scheme (CLSS) under PMAY‑U links interest subsidies to formal banking channels; women without independent credit histories or collateral face higher rejection rates. The Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (MoHUA) issued a 2020 advisory urging states to issue joint titles in the names of both spouses, but compliance remains uneven. A pilot initiative in Maharashtra demonstrating joint titling reported improved loan approval rates for women within two years. However, the lack of a mandatory gender‑audit for PMAY‑U project approvals means that many municipal corporations continue to allocate houses to male‑headed households by default. Integrating a gender‑responsive allocation matrix — weighting female‑headed households, survivors of domestic violence, and women with disabilities — into the state‑level Annual Implementation Plans would operationalise the Directive Principle of Article 39(a) and Supreme Court jurisprudence recognising the right to adequate housing as part of the right to life under Article 21.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: “Which provision of PMAY‑U most directly addresses women’s tenure insecurity in informal settlements?” Options include (a) Credit‑Linked Subsidy Scheme, (b) Beneficiary‑Led Construction, (c) In‑situ Slum Redevelopment, (d) Affordable Housing in Partnership. The correct answer is (c) In‑situ Slum Redevelopment, because it provides for on‑site rehabilitation with joint titling, whereas the other verticals require prior land ownership or formal credit access.
In‑situ Slum Redevelopment (ISSR) under PMAY‑U mandates that rehabilitated dwellings be allotted in the name of the woman head of household or jointly with the spouse, directly tackling tenure insecurity. The other verticals either presuppose land title (BLC) or rely on market‑based credit (CLSS), which women in informal settlements rarely possess.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question links a housing scheme to gender outcomes, first identify the vertical that includes a joint‑titling or women‑priority clause. Memorise the four PMAY‑U verticals and their eligibility triggers; the ISSR vertical is the only one that explicitly addresses tenure for women in slums.

Gendered Effects of Urban Air Quality Policies on Women's Reproductive Health

Urban air pollution policies such as the National Clean Air Programme (NCAP), launched in 2019, set city‑level PM2.5 reduction targets but rarely incorporate gender‑specific health endpoints. Epidemiological studies indicate that pregnant women exposed to sustained high PM2.5 levels face significantly higher risks of low birth weight and preterm delivery. Women in informal settlements, who rely on biomass cookstoves and live near traffic corridors, experience compounded indoor and outdoor exposure. The Supreme Court’s 1997 judgment in M.C. Mehta v. Union of India directed the conversion of Delhi’s public transport to compressed natural gas, yet the order did not mandate gender‑disaggregated monitoring of health outcomes. The NCAP’s city action plans include “source apportionment studies” but lack a requirement for reproductive health surveillance. A community‑based air‑quality monitoring initiative in Kanpur demonstrated increased antenatal care engagement following real‑time alerts. Embedding a “Gender‑Health Impact Assessment” module within the NCAP’s mid‑term review, and linking it to the Pradhan Mantri Surakshit Matritva Abhiyan (PMSMA) data platform, would enable targeted interventions such as clean‑cooking subsidies for pregnant women in high‑pollution wards.

Urban Governance and the Role of Women's Self‑Help Groups in Poverty Alleviation

Women’s Self‑Help Groups (SHGs) under the Deendayal Antyodaya Yojana – National Urban Livelihoods Mission (DAY‑NULM) have emerged as pivotal actors in urban poverty alleviation. As of recent years, over 2 million urban SHGs were registered, covering approximately 25‑30 million women. These groups facilitate micro‑credit through the Revolving Fund and the Community Investment Fund, enabling members to start or expand enterprises such as garment stitching, food vending, and waste‑recycling cooperatives. However, the SHG model faces structural constraints: (i) the Revolving Fund ceiling of ₹1 lakh per group limits scale‑up; (ii) bank linkage rates remain low in many states because of stringent KYC norms and lack of collateral; (iii) governance capacity varies widely, with only a small fraction of SHGs having trained book‑keepers. The Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs’ 2022 “SHG Strengthening Framework” envisages digital ledger integration via the e‑SHG portal, capacity‑building grants, and a gender‑responsive audit mechanism. Evidence from the Tamil Nadu Urban Livelihoods Mission suggests that SHGs linked to the “Mahalir Thittam” programme achieved significantly higher income growth compared to non‑linked groups over a three‑year period. Scaling such convergent models — combining SHG credit, skill certification under the Recognition of Prior Learning (RPL) scheme, and access to municipal vending zones — would translate collective savings into sustainable livelihood pathways for urban poor women.

Gendered Dimensions of Urban Public Space Design and Women's Safety

The design of urban public spaces — streets, parks, transit nodes, and marketplaces — shapes women’s mobility, economic participation, and perception of safety.
